profitability and electricity data, pool behavior, events, technical Q&A with answers,
and links to external resources.

**[#162 · 2024-02-26 08:59 · econoalchemist]**

Broadly speaking, the foundation is an initiative for Bitcoin mining & energy education and development. Rod & I recognized that while projects like OpenSats are doing wonderful work, there was enough room for an initiative  laser focused on mining. We are structuring it as a non-profit where all work product is public domain and/or FOSS to make it a win/win/win for everybody. 

More specifically:

1) As a non-profit, donations are tax deductible so mining companies can contribute to the initiative and benefit from the developments that come from it while reducing their tax burden and ensuring developments that help streamline operations and increase efficiencies keep getting pushed forward without having to spin up their own proprietary R&D lab. The devs who complete grant programs will be prime candidates for larger companies to acquire talent with experience and hands-on knowledge. 

2) Developers who are passionate about Bitcoin mining and energy who want to make open source contributions will have opportunities through the Foundation's grants to get the support they need to focus all their time & energy on projects they believe in. We are creating grant opportunities that will enable developers to have their housing and income covered while they spend several months on-site at our mining lab focusing on their project of choice. There will also be a business coaching/development component to help the devs commercialize their project and become their own entrepreneur and/or help them network with other miners looking to hire. 

3) Rod & I are building our own mining site in TN. We have a creative approach and without revealing too many details (yet), suffice it to say we think energy recovery from otherwise wasted resources and building a lucrative business that also just so happens to enable us to mine Bitcoin is going to be a grand slam for us. We have decided to notch out part of our mining site as the dedicated R&D space for the mining lab. Devs will be equipped with the full range of tools and resources we have on-site to experiment with their projects at scale. Having this initiative take place on our own mining site gives us a first look into these bleeding edge developments and because everything gets published as FOSS or a public doamain contribution, anyone mining Bitcoin will be able to benefit by incorporating these developments into their own operations.

The projects could range widely from miner management software, writing a curtailment protocol with high-speed resolution, developing competitive hardware on an open standard, one-click deployable pool servers, helping push Stratumv2 forward, developing new processes and standards for energy recovery, possibly even access to an SMR if talks with the TVA keep going well. The list can be extended.

But that's the idea in a nutshell.

🔥11
❤2
❤‍🔥1

**[#312 · 2024-03-03 10:37 · Michael Schmid @Schnitzel]**

+1 on the list.
Happy to help steer the "UI for pyasic" and "FOSS miner management" software.
My feeling is that HomeAssistant can be the UI for pyasic through hass-miner. Basically thats what it is doing today. Yea there could be a lot more improvements (more miner support, autodiscovery, better integrations into thermostats, a lot more documentation and examples). But I feel the ground work is laid for it and probably can be continued with that tech stack.

If we talk about Miner Management we probably have to find something else than  HomeAssistant (or build from scratch), as HomeAssistant is great if you have a couple of miners, but if you get to 100+ miners it will become probably unusable. So we need to build something that can handle the masses. BUT: we should use pyasic as the base for it. As pyasic standardizes how you talk to a miner through a unified API and you need that for systems of 2 or 2000 miners.

We would need to make sure though that the two tools have a clear differentiation, so maybe we should start to write some requirements analysis to make sure we understand what each tool does.
And yes my fiat job is Head of Product, so I think in these things all day long :)

❤1
🔥1

**[#313 · 2024-03-03 10:40 · econoalchemist]**

**[#317 · 2024-03-03 10:46 · Michael Schmid @Schnitzel]**

Yes, but it simplifies the way you talk to the miner dramatically, like with braiins, some things you can only change with accessing the miner through SSH and change the conf file, while other things you can change through the Telnet API. This is standardized my pyasic, so you just have a python SDK you talk too, and pyasic does the rest.
Also it standardizes across all miner types, so you just tell pyasic to "stop" mining, and pyasic then will know if and how the specific miner you have does allow to stop mining.

But yes obviously it cannot create a feature that the miner itself cant do, like it cannot set Wattage on Antminer Stock Firmware, as the firmware itself doesn't support it. But pyasic will give you a nice error message about it :)

**[#318 · 2024-03-03 10:49 · Skot Bitaxe]**

That’s what I was getting at — Pyasic can only support whatever (limited) feature set the proprietary FW decides to expose. And we know this changes a lot, especially on vendor stock FW

yea, agree, the very best would be to have our own Firmware specifically built with the usecases in mind (home heat reusage, grid stabilizing like Gridless needs it, etc.). And I could imagine a world were everyone uses the FOSS firmware and we technically don't need pyasic anymore. But I think until we are there it will take a little bit time and until then pyasic makes a LOT of stuff easier.

**[#539 · 2024-03-21 21:42 · Tyler Stevens]**

Evening Gents.

Joined this chat a week or so ago and have been lurking. Lots of fantastic ideas. We all appreciate the great work @ Eco and Rod. 

I wanted to throw an idea out there for mining for heat to help decentralize hash:

Anyone developing or working on chips / hashboards designed to run at higher operating temperatures? I know John at Future Bit mentioned some high temperature chip prototypes. Cracking that 100° C fluid outlet temp opens the door for heat reuse possibilities in dual phase systems. 

More delta_T = more useful, but usually = less efficient. I think theres room for development here. I don't care about 17.5 J/Th if I'm using it in my water heater. I care about enough bticoin subsidy to reduce my heating bill.

FOSS miners and firmware will crack open the door for integrated mining for heat systems. 

All the incentives are aligned. LFG!

Can't wait for the rip with @zbomzz (https://t.me/zbomzz) to drop.

Cheers!

💯1

**[#540 · 2024-03-21 21:47 · econoalchemist]**

Thanks Tyler, you'll appreciate @zbomzz (https://t.me/zbomzz)'s response to this question on this week's Pod256 episode. To summarize, the way I understand it is that less efficient chips like in the 7nm range could handle higher heat better but as the channels have gotten smaller like 3nm, the higher temps actually cause cross-channel bleed and disrupt the chips functionality. Zack explains it a lot better on the show.

**[#549 · 2024-03-22 11:30 · Zack Bomsta]**

Fun fact! Braiins once designed and built several hundred mining rigs for Marathon. The rigs used Bitmain S17 chips (BM1397) on an aluminum PCB. The custom FW that Braiins built for these rigs ran them super hot. The "dangerous temp" by default was set to 110C if I remember correctly. The larger 7nm chip in conjunction with the aluminum PCB made it so these things could run HOT. How do I know this? Because Braiins donated them to Unbound hoping we could find a use for them. 

They're efficiency of ~45J/TH isn't great by today's standards, but to paraphrase your earlier point, efficiency isn't paramount in heat recapture systems. 

I think it would be very feasible to design an aluminum PCB with S9 or S17 chips—any chip that uses a 7nm process or higher, really—specifically for high temp immersion heat recapture. Sounds like a good candidate for a 256foundation grant.

**[#737 · 2024-04-06 13:55 · Brett Rowan]**

The whole idea of “pooling” is just that you get an average of what you would get from the whole block over time based on your hashrate.

So if you have 1% of the network hashrate for example, and you pool with a pool that has 49% (with you making it 50%), they would find ~50% of the blocks, and you would get 2% of each block (100/50), but only half the blocks on the network, meaning you get paid 2% of a block every 2 blocks vs 100% of a block every 100 blocks.  Same thing, but more gradually.

**[#738 · 2024-04-06 13:56 · Expatriotic]**

**[#811 · 2024-04-18 14:31 · Tyler Stevens]**

Is physical (hash location) or digital (pool templates) centralization a larger current threat to the network? What do you all think?

**[#812 · 2024-04-18 14:31 · Alex Brammer]**

pool templates = censorship risk based centralization

**[#814 · 2024-04-18 14:32 · Alex Brammer]**

pool stratum = centralization risk wrt 51% attack (controlling hashrate)

**[#817 · 2024-04-18 14:33 · Alex Brammer]**

although, i suppose the template could also be related to 51% attack depending on how it's used. If its used to mine on an invalid chain to roll it back, that could be related to the stratum (where the hash is flowing from the physical mines) risk vector.

**[#818 · 2024-04-18 14:33 · Tyler Stevens]**

Yea, the digital threat takes literally way less effort to execute

**[#819 · 2024-04-18 14:35 · Jstefanop]**

Control of block creation and censorship is a way bigger threat

❤1

**[#820 · 2024-04-18 14:37 · Zack Bomsta]**

I think there’s a third, and that’s legal entity centralization. Too much hash under control of a single legal entity.

🔥2

**[#822 · 2024-04-18 14:38 · Alex Brammer]**

i think this is probably similar to pool centralization

**[#823 · 2024-04-18 14:39 · Jstefanop]**

It’s all the same thing at this point, majority of the pools, Bitmain and public cos work together

**[#826 · 2024-04-18 14:42 · Alex Brammer]**

Jay from Foundry is likely the world's leading expert on this - asking him right now (viz whether template centralization = risk of 51% attack or just risk of censorship)

**[#827 · 2024-04-18 14:45 · Jstefanop]**

51 attacks are fud vestiges of the early days of mining when it was more plausible, no one can successfully reorg the chain long enough to cause damage at this point

👏1

**[#828 · 2024-04-18 14:46 · Alex Brammer]**

if a pool that controlled 51% of hashrate through its stratums was funded by someone with unlimited ability to print money to fund the ongoing cost of it, are you saying this isn't feasible?

**[#829 · 2024-04-18 14:47 · Alex Brammer]**

and if they were doing it with proxy-pool front ends so it wasn't obvious to most miners that they actually controlled that hashrate, couldn't they do it for long enough to call the integrity of the chain into question?

🙈1

**[#831 · 2024-04-18 14:51 · Jstefanop]**

Not really 51% is kinda of a misconception, in order to double spend you would have to confirm the transactions faster than the entire network

**[#833 · 2024-04-18 14:52 · Jstefanop]**

Which is why it’s not really technically feasible to reorg with how massive the bitcoin network is now you would need like 1000EH to do it successfully

**[#835 · 2024-04-18 14:58 · Jstefanop]**

Plus it’s a cool game theory IMO even if all the top pools/miners colluded (like they are now) why would they try and double spend or do something stupid to ruin their reputation…they’ll do exactly what they are doing right now which is control of the network through template creation

**[#836 · 2024-04-18 14:58 · Alex Brammer]**

agree with this, with the exception of full coercive state capture

**[#837 · 2024-04-18 14:59 · Alex Brammer]**

but if what you're saying is correct and it's not feasible for a 51% attack to occur at this network size, then it's also not feasible for a state to capture the amount of hashrate required to overcome the incentive alignment that is keeping the network safe at this point.

**[#838 · 2024-04-18 15:02 · Jstefanop]**

It’s not impossible just much harder, and centralization forces are a bigger concern around censorship threat than an outright attack

**[#970 · 2024-05-19 09:28 · Michael Schmid @Schnitzel]**

Soo funny enough I kinda started to work on this: https://freedomtech.hosting 
The idea of Freedomtech is thats its a combination model of Voltage (everything in the cloud, nothing local, managed for you) and Umbrel/start9 (nothing in the cloud, all local and you are by yourself).
Freedomtech will allow you to start in the cloud, and eventually move to a local server that is still maintained and managed by the Freedomtech Team but locally, so all the date still belongs to you and cant be rugged by Freedomtech.
The website lists lightning/nostr/fedimints as they are more understood by the broad bitcoin community, but the roadmap also has miningpools on it. Unfortunately the project stalled a bit as our twins are arriving soon and my wife will have lower income, so I took a bigger job at my fiat work to get some more $$$ which also makes me super busy and not much time for Bitcoin side projects. Maybe I should apply to opensats (or 256 foundation) to be able to focus fully on FreedomtechHosting and NakamotoHeating 🧐 - just listened to your RHR pod @skot9000 (https://t.me/skot9000) and gave me a glimpse to maybe pursue this.

**[#1164 · 2024-08-09 15:48 · econoalchemist]**

Fair enough. Foundry isn't suggesting or requesting anything from us so I'm hard pressed to see how the lobbying analogy applies here. 

For what it's worth, there might be a couple things we've implied but just need to be stated explicitly for clarity's sake: nobody gets to tell the devs what they work on, they decide that for themselves. The Foundation formalizes a plan with the devs for their project and helps them manage executing. Donations are 100% pass through to the devs for their projects. 

Everybody benefits from free and open development. Microsoft finally capitulated and acknowledged this in 2016 when they joined the Linux Foundation. The 256 Foundation gives anyone (even big companies) a mechanism to support open source development, that will ultimately help solve the problems they face in their own organizations, while also creating a tax deductible incentive to do so.

**[#1560 · 2025-01-31 03:43 · jungly 🐕💸]**

How do we envision hydrapool to manage payouts?

We will do PPLNS, however, as number of miners grow how do we manage payouts to them if they don't fit in the coinbase?

PPLNS centralised pools manage this by taking custody of the earnings and then dispersing funds later to miners. Do we want to do the same?

**[#1561 · 2025-01-31 03:45 · jungly 🐕💸]**

There are a couple of other possibilities

1. Pay over LN immediately, so we don't custody funds for too long
2. Allow atomic swaps of shares for BTC and let the buyers of the shares later get payouts? IDK, if this fits into hydrapool's model.
3. Look into other custodial solutions using ecash?

Curious where we want go with this, and if we have already have a inkling of what to do for an MVP?

**[#1562 · 2025-01-31 05:38 · Karl]**

I don't know what a hydrapool is but if what I'm imagining is what you're talking about, then a federated ecash mint where those with the most hash are the federated members would make sense. You could combine that concept with something like Super Testnet's bitpac to allow for a Treasury to be managed by the largest contributors to the pool, jobs delegated ect. Pool contributors that show consistent and significant commitment to the pool could gain a key in the multisig.

**[#1563 · 2025-01-31 05:39 · jungly 🐕💸]**

What you mention is also very similar to the radpool design: https://radpool.xyz

**[#1564 · 2025-01-31 05:39 · Karl]**

https://github.com/supertestnet/bitpac-smart-contracts

**[#1600 · 2025-02-02 18:33 · econoalchemist]**

At first I was thinking it would be solo only, meant to run as the pool server on an Ember One complete mining system, or VPS, or self hosted hardware. But after the NEMS summit, I'm kind of thinking ecash is the way to go.

**[#1601 · 2025-02-02 18:36 · econoalchemist]**

We turned off the self-hosted pool after the Telehash, but yes you can get a tax deductible receipt for donated hash. I don't think it's based on your cost but based on what the foundation earns. I'll have to check with someone else to verify.

🤔2

**[#1605 · 2025-02-03 08:43 · econoalchemist]**

Correct me if I'm wrong but I thought the number of participants in a coin base payout was limited only because of Bitmain's firmware. In other words, if we built the pool in such a way that it used PPLNS and paid direct from coin base (like Laurentian Pool) then there is no custody involved. The trade off is Antminers are incompatible.

**[#1607 · 2025-02-03 08:47 · jungly 🐕💸]**

You are absolutely right.

Apart from the incompatibility with antminers, we also have a concern that the coinbase can eat into valuable blockspace. 

Depending on the fee market this can be problematic. Say we have 5000 miners on the pool, we could eat a fair about of blockspace. Things will work, but other pools will collect higher payouts.

Having said all that, a first MVP to directly pay from coinbase is a valid approach. We can improve this as community develops new ideas, they can be federation (fedipool), ecash (hashpool), lightning atomic swaps (p2poolv2) etc.

❤1
🔥1
💯1

**[#1608 · 2025-02-03 09:15 · econoalchemist]**

Yeah, I think the play is to build the pool specifically for free and open Bitcoin mining hardware like the Bitaxe and soon to be Ember One. I think the majority of users from the Bitaxe & Ember One camps will run their hardware regardless of fee revenue, fee revenues will be low for some period of time IMO, and like you said we can ship a new mining pool format and iterate on it later.

💯2

**[#1853 · 2025-04-14 16:51 · Yooper Hodl]**

AI’s explanation is better then mine:

A "hotel fee" in Vnish mining firmware refers to a function that allows a portion of a miner's hashrate to be redirected to pay for services such as electricity, equipment colocation (hosting), or miner maintenance. This feature is particularly useful for mining hotels or colocation companies, as it enables them to automatically collect rent or service fees from their clients by diverting a set percentage of the mining output directly to their own pool or wallet. (https://vnish-firmware.com/en/)
Key points about the hotel fee in Vnish firmware:
The hotel fee is an option to redirect part of the hashrate for payment of operational costs like electricity or hosting, rather than having the miner pay these fees separately. (https://vnish.ru/)
This function is often used by mining hotels to ensure timely and guaranteed rent collection from clients. (https://vnish-firmware.com/en/)
The hotel fee is separate from the standard developer fee (devfee), which is typically 1.8–2.8% of the total hashrate and goes to the Vnish developers. (https://vnish.group/en/faq-vnish-en/)
As of recent firmware versions (e.g., 1.2.6-rc5), the hotel fee function has been removed from the standard Vnish firmware, but custom or partner versions can be built to include it for specific clients or hosting companies. (https://vnish.group/en/faq-vnish-en/)
The hotel fee can be customized in partner versions, allowing mining hotels to set their own percentage and direct the fee to their chosen pool or wallet. (https://vnish-firmware.com/en/)
In summary, the hotel fee in Vnish firmware is a customizable feature for mining operations to automate the collection of service fees by redirecting a portion of mining rewards, but it is not present in the latest standard firmware and is now available only in custom builds for partners. (https://vnish.group/en/faq-vnish-en/)
Citations:
https://vnish.group/en/faq-vnish-en/ (https://vnish.group/en/faq-vnish-en/)
https://vnish-firmware.com/en/ (https://vnish-firmware.com/en/)
https://vnish.ru (https://vnish.ru/)
https://www.reddit.com/r/litecoinmining/comments/ojkebn/in_the_nicehash_custom_firmware_for_the_l3_theres/ (https://www.reddit.com/r/litecoinmining/comments/ojkebn/in_the_nicehash_custom_firmware_for_the_l3_theres/)
https://vnish.com.ru (https://vnish.com.ru/)
https://vnish-firmware.com/en/razgon-firmware-vnish-antminer-l9/ (https://vnish-firmware.com/en/razgon-firmware-vnish-antminer-l9/)
https://www.nicehash.com/blog/post/nicehash-firmware-update-hashrate-splitting-now-available (https://www.nicehash.com/blog/post/nicehash-firmware-update-hashrate-splitting-now-available)

Answer from Perplexity: https://www.perplexity.ai/search/what-is-a-hotel-fee-in-vnish-m-9E5AnP4cTLmyepwJz5lhHg?utm_source=copy_output (https://www.perplexity.ai/search/what-is-a-hotel-fee-in-vnish-m-9E5AnP4cTLmyepwJz5lhHg?utm_source=copy_output)

**[#2177 · 2025-05-16 01:37 · Ryan]**

emberOne is not intended to compete with Antminer hash boards on price. Instead, it is an open-source reference to be used as a guide—or directly used—by engineers designing mining hardware with characteristics and form factors *distinct* from Antminer hash boards. This includes applications beyond industrial-scale, shoebox-on-a-shelf mining. Keep in mind that future models of emberOne don't use Bitmain chips; instead, they use chips from new vendors who are directly selling their chips to hardware producers. The emberOne series is blazing the trail for a new category of mining systems.

❤2

**[#2178 · 2025-05-16 01:44 · Ryan]**

Mujina's scope (and the control board's scope) is a bit broader, in that it will eventually be used with all kinds of hardware, not just emberOne.

❤1

**[#2179 · 2025-05-16 02:06 · Bas | Mining Wholesale 🇳🇱]**

Thanks for the explanation. I understand the incentive for the emberOne, but not the prioritization in comparison to the Adit. Libre + Mujina + Adit + native boards would be a massive market, because of the economic incentive.

**[#2180 · 2025-05-16 07:44 · econoalchemist]**

The 256 Foundation is a nonprofit; this isn't about how big the market is, this is about building something Bitmain doesn't control.

🔥5

**[#2181 · 2025-05-16 11:27 · Reckless Apotheosis]**

Also, for the $/Th, when the ASICs are free, it becomes a lot easier to compete with old 19J hashboards. And, buying EmberOnes and LibreBoards from community members continues to support innovation in this space. Something we dearly need.

👍1

**[#2182 · 2025-05-16 12:31 · Ryan]**

You raise a good point about the Adit + Antminer use case. The good news—the beauty of the 256 open-source project—is that we're building an entire ecosystem, not just one-off products. While we're starting with support for 256's own hardware, emberOne, the foundational work in Mujina and Libre creates a platform for all hardware implementations.

Mujina is deliberately modular and hashboard-agnostic. Every advancement we make directly benefits integrations via Adit, custom boards with new chips, hardware products we haven't even imagined yet, and perhaps even mega-mining.

The Mujina + Libre + Adit + existing boards combination definitely has potential. As our community grows and we open the project for contributions, developers passionate about Adit and other hardware can accelerate support for these use cases if they need more attention. With each new contribution, the entire ecosystem grows stronger.

**[#2183 · 2025-05-16 12:51 · Mike Hamilton]**

I see your point and fully agree on the supporting the innovation.  But I disagree with the broader concept on getting close to pricing of s19/older boards.  Even with free BZM2s, smallish-ish scale (in the range of 3000-10000 machines) manufacturing of 3 hashboards + control board + PSU + Fans + Shell is between $8-$12/TH with (free) BZM2s (appx 400 GH/s per chip).  The economies of scale that Bitmain has in manufacturing is beyond our wildest dreams.  Probably a whole order of magnitude, meaning they're probably manufacturing/assembling machines for between $.50-$1.50/TH (not including asics).  

The real benefit that the foundation and their projects provide is the ability to create alternative hardware that can be customized for different use cases and in different form factors (e.g. a home water heater, pool heater, beer brewing boiler, DFRs, etc).

🔥2

## 2025-06

**[#2305 · 2025-06-03 17:55 · Scott Offord]**

**[#2356 · 2025-06-05 15:25 · jungly 🐕💸]**

The catch is that ckpool is very hard to extend. If you want to build any interesting accounting or payout mechanisms you have to deal with a lot of proxy, node etc stuff ck has built in C over the last decade.

Datum is tied to the ocean ecosystem. It is much simpler code than ckpool, so one could fork it. But if you are not aligned with upstream, you'll have a big pain keeping yourself up to date.

We are instead building a simple stratum server in rust with extensibility as one of the main goals. We ourselves want to build at least two accounting and payout mechanisms, if you can call them that. Solo and PPLNS.

Hydrapool

- easy to run
- easy to extend and experiment with

❤1

**[#2523 · 2025-06-18 08:14 · AgentP]**

One thought: a lot of cases, both stock and heater cases like the Stealth Miner, only expose a single board edge to the user without disassembling the case.  It might be worth thinking about which ports/buttons/SD cards, etc. would be good to have easily accessible to the user.  For example, if I'm flashing new firmware on my space heater, it'd be nice not to have to take apart the whole thing just to reach the SD card or hold a reset button.

**[#2524 · 2025-06-18 08:29 · econoalchemist]**

**[#2526 · 2025-06-18 08:32 · Michael Schmid @Schnitzel]**

yep that is the exact thought as Eco mentioned. Only difference is that we don't have a lot of space on only one edge, so we ended up having two edges that include user accesible ports (front and top - see the github issue)

**[#2643 · 2025-07-02 13:31 · econoalchemist]**

Hey everybody, I have an update on the first batch order for the Ember One hashboards. We decided it would be best to build a prototype of the v4 board since there were so many changes from v3 and validate that everything on v4 is working as intended before we do the small batch production run (which turned out not being that small!). 

We were originally aiming for placing the order before the end of June and at this time, it looks like we will be placing the order for the small batch production run within the next 10 to 12 days. Now aiming for mid-July. Skot has all parts on-hand and has started the prototype assembly process.

Once we place the order, we should see the finished units turned around within 4-weeks. So right now, it is looking like these will start shipping to you in mid-August. 

Reminder, this is for the hashboards only and does not include power supply, heat-sink, cooling fan, firmware, or control board. All of those things are in the works so the timing should work out well, the way things are looking now.

**[#2654 · 2025-07-03 01:14 · Mike Hamilton]**

S17’s did it.  Any time you factory reverted or upgraded firmware, the MAC would change.  I think some of the s19’s also did it, but I know there were like 3 or 4 different control boards for the early s19 series (during covid).  microBT has had hardcoded MACs since at least the 3x series, and I would guess earlier.  

But I wouldn’t necessarily call them rotating mac’s.  The address was defined in a text file that wasn’t in the folders that were preserved during upgrades or reverts.

**[#2655 · 2025-07-03 01:18 · Mike Hamilton]**

I used to use a Perl daemon that I was able to drop on our old s17’s  (because the Bitmain firmware had Perl installed)

**[#2758 · 2025-08-06 09:53 · csh2000]**

Thanks, will try again this week.

Will the pool support connecting to your own node? If so, will it be possible to run the node in pruned mode?

**[#2759 · 2025-08-06 10:03 · jungly 🐕💸]**

Yes, totally you will be able to connect to your own node. That's one of the key features of Hydrapool.

However, mining with pruned nodes is not supported by bitcoin Core. It has to do with the getblocktemplate API not supported by Core for a pruned node. The reason for it is to do with building templates. We need to check make sure we select transactions whose inputs are not spent yet, for that, we need to check for the absence of the inputs in spent outputs set, in all history. IIRC

**[#2887 · 2025-08-16 07:15 · Skot Bitaxe]**

It’s the same as everyone else, as far as I can tell. The heatsinks are sitting on top of ~0.5mm chips. We saw 96 chip and 120 chip models.

The goal is good, flat, even pressure from the heatsinks on the chips. It’s tough because you are trying to cover so many individually soldered chips with a big piece of aluminum, and everything is expanding and contracting with big heat changes.. I feel like a dust skirt could be done though.. is that a big problem for you?

**[#2902 · 2025-08-16 17:21 · Mæstro Juergen]**

**[#2903 · 2025-08-16 17:33 · Mæstro Juergen]**

Thinking of something trying something called a gap filler pad available in sizes smaller than 0.5 mm for a reusable heat sink I can use to test repair boards nonce numbers before getting them all messy with fujipoly. Useful when I often have to go in for a revision.

**[#2904 · 2025-08-16 17:38 · Skot Bitaxe]**

Interesting. I always thought those pads had poor thermal conductivity?

**[#2905 · 2025-08-16 17:44 · Mæstro Juergen]**

Gap filler pad is hella squishy so you a lot less Gap then what's on the label, I theorize it should be small enough that at 7.5wmk it should still perform better than 3wmk Fuji poly and not dry out in 6 months either, might even be the right move for immersion mining. 7.5 is maybe only twice the cost of paste but is available in 10 and 13 wmk also if you want to spend $200 on a new machine and just do the conversion right away.

**[#2906 · 2025-08-16 17:49 · Mæstro Juergen]**

Then there's graphite pad which really isn't compressible at all but has wmk of over 40. And finally there is phase change pad that which gets gooey over a few dozen Celsius, it will be my second material to test. I wish I could get a low cost silicone pad at 0.3 mm at a high enough wmk to make up for the distance but that's prohibitively expensive.

**[#2983 · 2025-08-27 17:18 · econoalchemist]**

Hi everybody, I've got another update on the Ember One hashboards initial order: Since the last update on July 20, Skot was able to get the issue resolved with the ASIC chips communicating and he is expecting to have the validation process done this week. Once we have v4 fully validated then we can start the production process. 

Since so much time has elapsed since we initially spoke with Ben about producing these units, I followed up with him today to see if he still could fulfill the order and if the price has changed since May. After our conversation, we decided that this batch order of Ember Ones will come from the new assembly line I'm building. I was planning on building 100 units anyways so all of those will now go towards this order. It does not look like I can exactly match Ben's original price from May of $150/unit + $500 setup fee. But I need some more time to figure out what my price is, I'm going to try my best to stay as close to the original quote as possible. As far as lead-time goes, I need more time to figure it out as I'm learning the manufacturing process as I go. Stay tuned for more info, I already have all the ASIC chips on hand and for those who don't know already, I bought a pick & place machine to assemble the hardware we are designing at 256 Foundation.

Reminder, this is for the hashboards only and does not include power supply, heat-sink, cooling fan, firmware, or control board. All of those things are in the works though with more info to follow.

Here is what I have for orders so far, if you want to modify your order, please let me know. I'll start collecting payments once the units have been made and are ready to ship.

**[#3053 · 2025-09-18 10:08 · csh2000]**

Hello, I heard you discussing hashboard heating in the last two episodes. I’d like to share my experience with the jPro hashboards we use in the Nocoiner miner (1HB Loki miner). 

When a miner enters sleep mode, hashboards with a PIC shut down and stop generating heat. Hashboards without a PIC keep the VRM active: the chips stay hot even when they’re not mining. This means you’re fully dependent on the fans; if they fail, overheating occurs, power consumption rises, and eventually the miner itself fails. PSU trips, plastic melts… I had several episodes of this before I figured out the problem.

👍3
🔥1

**[#3115 · 2025-10-15 15:18 · csh2000]**

Hi @tylerkstevens (https://t.me/tylerkstevens), I heard you talking about Home Assistant.
We’re working on a software that runs on a "RPi+small screen" to do three things (for now):

1. Provide internet to the miner — it connects to Wi-Fi and shares the connection through the Ethernet port. Shows some basic information in the screen (hashrate, temp, btc price,...).

2. Enable remote monitoring and control of the miner via Telegram.

3. Integrate the miner with Home Assistant, with everything pre-installed so the user only needs to enter the miner’s IP address, create automations, and build their dashboard.

Future plans include (hopefully, somehow) integrating this into the LibreBoard — so we’re doing some groundwork in advance — and adding more services such as a node, pool, and others.

Hopefully, we’ll have the first beta next month.

**[#3319 · 2025-11-13 14:56 · Dean]**

Hey everyone, I’d love your input on a decentralized Bitcoin mining pool/protocol idea I’m thinking through. Here are the key points:

Decentralized Share Tracking: Use a some sort of sidechain or network where every mining share is logged in a distributed, transparent ledger. This may not even be a blockchain, but some data set. That way, no central party tracks shares, it's all recorded by the side network.

Multi-Sig Payout System: Each node operator on the sidechain holds a private key. Since only a certain number of bitcoin addresses can be in the coinbase transaction, the remaining bitcoin amount needs to be stored and distributed. For every Bitcoin block, the side network creates a new multi-sig wallet composed of all those node's private keys. A certain percentage of those keys (say 51%) must sign off to move any leftover rewards. This means each block’s leftover funds go into a new multi-sig controlled by the current set of nodes, preventing any single point of failure or lock-up. Would need to look into the limit of multisig private keys.

Each block, there would be a new multisig created based on current side network nodes. This multisig would only hold the leftover bitcoin from that current block reward. Then next block, it would either distribute funds to the hashers, or add the funds to the new next block multisig if there aren't enough funds to distribute. Would probably want to only be on side network nodes that have submitted valid shares to prevent an attack of nodes adding to the multisig private key set and never releasing the coins. If you are submitting shares, you have an interest in getting a payout.

Stratum V2 for Encryption and Miner Control: Use Stratum V2 to keep all communications encrypted and let miners choose their own block templates.

No Central Pool Operator: The whole system is decentralized. No central operator charging fees or setting rules, just the network of node operators and miners tracking work and distributing payouts directly from the Bitcoin network.

That’s the concept so far. Really needs smarter people than me to run with it. Open to suggestions on making it even better!

**[#3485 · 2025-11-20 23:46 · Ryan]**

I'll answer this a little indirectly:

Mujina as it exists today is a single program, mujina-miner, that runs on Linux. You have to "install" and run it on some existing Linux OS—anything from Omrachy on your laptop to Raspberry Pi OS on a Raspberry Pi. It will talk to hash boards via USB. For any system components that aren't directly a part of the hashboard, you're sort of on your own to integrate those electrically and software-wise.

LibreBoard will run Raspberry Pi OS out of the box, and so it fits into the above built-it-yourself model too. But the bigger goal is to create MujinaOS—a whole Linux OS that will run on LibreBoard (and eventually other control boards) with mujina-miner and everything else (kernel modifications, DTs, modules, perhaps other relevant userspace processes alongside mujine-miner, etc.) nicely integrated together as a single image install that supports all the quirks of the LibreBoard hardware, and provides an ideal platform, toolchain, etc. to add application-specific components—like a custom program that drives your system's sensors, actuators, and mujina-miner together to keep your hot tub warm.

**[#3700 · 2025-11-24 13:46 · Reckless Apotheosis]**

The expected response from the Intel® Blockscale™ 1000 Series ASIC (BZM2) when receiving the NOOP (No Operation) command depends slightly on the documentation used, though the purpose and format remain consistent.
The NOOP instruction uses Opcode 4'hF. It functions as a debug feature intended to confirm that the ASIC is synchronized with the host and has decoded its own ID.
The ASIC is expected to return 3 bytes of data.
Expected Response Data
The sources provide two slightly different ASCII results for the response:
1. "BZM": The ASIC Datasheet defines the expected response as the 24-bit value 24’h425A4D, which represents the Little Endian ASCII for “BZM”.
2. "BZ2": The High Level Architectural Specification and the system power-up documentation indicate that the ASIC always returns 3 bytes spelling "BZ2" in ASCII.
    ◦ During the system power-up sequence, the host specifically sends the "noop" command and the ASIC is expected to respond "BZ2" to complete the initial enumeration and initialization process

**[#3910 · 2025-12-09 14:18 · AgentP]**

That's where I started.  Gemeni helped me the rest of the way.  Here's the problems that tripped me up, if you're wanting to make those more robust:
1. Ubuntu 22.04 LTS (just what I had) doesn't have updated Rust in the repos (just 1.71 or something).  So I had to uninstall Rust (from apt and snap apparently), and reinstall using the script from the official Rust website to get the latest:
curl --proto '=https' --tlsv1.2 -sSf https://sh.rustup.rs | sh -s -- -y

2. First build failed because I didn't have updated OpenSSL dev libraries.  
sudo apt update
sudo apt install libssl-dev pkg-config

3.  Another build issue with clang:
sudo apt install clang libclang-dev

After that I got a successful build!

Re p2poolv2 code. There's a huge overlap between hydrapool and P2Poolv2. So a lot of the core libs, like stratum, accounting, coinbase etc are in the P2Poolv2 repo and used by hydrapool. I did think of moving them into an independent repo, but it was getting messy and I just punted for now.

**[#3916 · 2025-12-10 01:37 · Aaron]**

@jungly (https://t.me/jungly) Is Hydrapool the successor to p2poolv2 or a separate project? I see you've done past work on a variety of mining pool projects. Is this Hydrapool your latest / main focus?

**[#3917 · 2025-12-10 02:08 · jungly 🐕💸]**

I think they both go in tandem and serve very different niches.

Hydrapool is for users who want to run a pool for themselves or their network of miners. One can even take hydrapool, run it as it is to provide a pool with a pplns payout, or change the accounting/payout mechanisms to run a fork with their choice of accounting/payouts etc. 

P2Poolv2 is rebooting the old p2pool, building on some of the new changes in bitcoin that will help us solve the problems that the original p2pool faced. P2Poolv2 is more opinionated in terms of payouts/accounting etc as it has to solve a very specific problem of providing a decentralised pool that can scale. 

I think both projects serve different needs. We are hoping to build and maintain them in tandem. That's why it was important to reuse code as libraries and not fork, if that makes sense.

❤3

**[#3946 · 2025-12-13 17:47 · AgentP]**

@jungly (https://t.me/jungly) 
It compiles!  I haven't tested yet.  Maybe tonight.  If it goes we'll, I can have a PR in next week maybe.  
I kept changes to p2poolv2_lib to an absolute minimum, and most of the logic is in hydrapool.  I went with a file based approach:
If p2poolv2 config contains a value for payout_file_path, then the function "build_output_distribution" (in http://notify.rs) loads the payouts from that file.  If that option doesn't exist, or the payout list has errors (pays too much, bad addresses, etc.) then it defaults to the previous PPLNS.  
Reading from a file is a LOT faster and simpler than an API fetch (which is still easy).  <100 lines of code. 

Hydrapool contains the logic for periodically fetching payouts from the API and writing them to the file.  Again, set by config option "downstream_payout_url".  

So that should make it easy for people to put whatever logic they want to in hydrapool, or any other program, and write those payouts to a local file or serve them remotely.  Ultimate flexibility and customizability without any extra burden on the p2poolv2 developers.

**[#4034 · 2026-01-04 13:39 · Ryan]**

Looks like it’s failing the first time the application itself is trying to talk to the board via USB. The initial communication up through discovery is done by the OS.

So, maybe the user mujina is running as doesn’t have permissions to the USB device?

**[#4035 · 2026-01-04 14:57 · Loren Lang]**

This is likely tricky as I think Mujina scans the USB bus to discover devices, but then uses the regular serial tty interfaces to talk to them requiring the appropriate Linux driver for that USB adapter. The serial ports should be accessible in WSL as COM1-10 should show up as /dev/ttyS0-9 inside WSL to my understanding, but this will appear as basic serial ports, not USB-to-Serial adapters like /dev/ttyACM0-9 are. Mujina could use them, but I think it's discovery mechanism would need to be modified to allow specifying them unless there is some way to manually configure them.

**[#4036 · 2026-01-04 15:01 · Loren Lang]**

Now, the other approach that might be easier would be to pass in the USB device into WSL. I have not done this yet, but take a look here: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/wsl/connect-usb

**[#4116 · 2026-01-05 17:41 · Ryan]**

This is awakening a few memory cells.

I had to move away from tokio-serial and write something custom, because it couldn't handle baud rate changes after it was in use (ownership issues after splitting tx and rx streams).

What I wrote was pretty POSIX specific, maybe even Linux specific. Perhaps that's why it's balking under WSL—the translation they do from Linux library and system calls down to the underlying Windows hardware isn't quite the same as being on pure Linux.

**[#4117 · 2026-01-05 17:42 · Ryan]**

See some more background here: https://github.com/256foundation/mujina/discussions/10

**[#4118 · 2026-01-05 17:56 · Ryan]**

s/Had to/Chose to/

There are other ways to skin this cat. Maybe it's better to return to a well-supported, platform-independent serial library and add a layer of indirection where single, dedicated task owns the port and provides channels that can be passed out to the ultimate users.

**[#4119 · 2026-01-05 18:05 · Loren Lang]**

If this is WSL2 (which I'm pretty sure it has to be as I don't think WSL1 supports USB passthrough at all), then it's using Hyper-V underneath and running a full Linux kernel optimized for the Hyper-V environment, but at the serial tty layer, it's standard Linux using the USB Serial CDC ACM driver. Now, there could be some odd filtering down in the USB layers, but it seems unlikely to me as this should just be standard USB Bulk pipes and Control requests. I'd spend some time debugging this, but I don't have my own Bitaxe yet and I'm still bringing up driver support for my custom board. If I can get some USB captures of it in the failing environment compared to the working environment, I might be able to find some clues on why it's not working.

**[#4120 · 2026-01-05 18:06 · Loren Lang]**

A  USB pcap capture with Wireshark from both Mujina starting up and attaching to the board in Windows and the same capture made on the Windows host, but with the device attached and running Mujina in Linux.

**[#4121 · 2026-01-05 18:08 · Loren Lang]**

If Wireshark doesn't work when the device is captured by WSL2, then an alternate method to capture as documented here might work: https://github.com/dorssel/usbipd-win/wiki/Troubleshooting#usb-capture

**[#4122 · 2026-01-05 18:31 · Ryan]**

And now that I'm at a computer and looking at this more closely, I think Mujina is giving you the best error it can from the application's perspective. It believes it's successfully talking out the serial port; however, it never receives a response, and thus times out. If it were getting an error from the OS, we'd be seeing that error instead.

I do see why @penguin359 (https://t.me/penguin359) is pointing us down the unknown-things-happening-within-WSL route. It's not clear yet whether and what data sent on /dev/tty-whatever ever shows up on the other end of the USB cable.

Unfortunately (fortunately?) I don't have a Windows machine here to test on.

OK, I did a test to see if USB Serial works in WSL2 and I see no issues with it. I don't have a Bitaxe, but I do have a different USB device that uses the CDC ACM protocol for USB Serial. I was able to forward it into a WSL2 environment with Ubuntu 24.04 and connect to /dev/ttyACM0.  I can see the data that my board is transmitting as long as I set the correct baud rate. **[#4174 · 2026-01-07 17:50 · Tyler Stevens]**

With hot swappable boards - or folks building software interfaces to define a max wattage of a heater for example - how will Mujina handle how much power goes to each board? 

@tronsington (https://t.me/tronsington) has been playing with LuxOS today, and its an interesting challenge for us to create a software interface that lets you command a “system” power limit, which selects the correct frequency and voltage profile to apply that power across however many boards you have.

**[#4175 · 2026-01-07 17:54 · Ryan]**

I was imagining a few things: (A) The user might want to specify a system-wide power and let Mujina decide how best (most efficiently) achieve that with the current set of boards. Or (B) The user might want to do that on a per-board (or per group of boards?) level because maybe they're on different cooling loops 🤷‍♂️, and then Mujina would take that as a constraint and do its best with the remainder of the system.

🔥1

**[#4176 · 2026-01-07 17:55 · Ryan]**

Obvioulsy a lot of this is yet to come, and your feedback about what you want it to do can inform what we write.

**[#4179 · 2026-01-07 17:56 · Tyler Stevens]**

They both make sense. B for when you have water blocks in series for example.

**[#4180 · 2026-01-07 17:57 · Ryan]**

But I do have this notion in there of a "scheduler" that sort of sits right in the middle of things wtih awareness of the hashboards, pools (splits, etc.), and power requests from the user (API)—and that scheduler would apply whatever policies we set and try to meet those kinds of goals as boards, pools, power targets, etc. come and go.

❤2

**[#4182 · 2026-01-07 18:06 · Dylan Seib]**

I was thinking about it like this, with Home Assistant and EmberOne in mind.
We know each EmberOne can run at 100W. Therefore in HA, the user would be able to set a power limit of 0*-100W OR the user would be able to set a percentage, 0*-100% for how much power to use. (*not really, but keep for our purposes). When Mujina detects a new board is added, it reports that to HA that a new board is added and the new power limit increases by 100W, eg the user can select 0*-200W now. The kicker is if the user sets 150W as the limit, Mujina would need to know to take the new set power limit and divide by the board count, setting each to 75W. This is mostly a half thought right now, but I think you can see where I'm going. I have no idea how this would be written.

**[#4422 · 2026-02-13 06:27 · PizzAndy]**

RE: Open source chips

This came up last night at my local meetup:
This might be an ignorant question, but is there any reason we *need* the chip packages to be of a certain size? Could we not just make them bigger to correspond to the increase size in the traces?

Our goals aren't necessarily to cram as many J/Th into as small of volume as possible. Could this size increase be done in tandem with exploring something like silicon carbide/gallium nitride to push our chip temps past that 100C mark?

🔥3

**[#4424 · 2026-02-13 17:44 · AgentP]**

I explored this with Grok a while back.  I think with older tech we might be able to do a small run of older chips for ~$20k (AI WAG, not a real quote).  Could likely get to 125C chips by optimizing for heat tolerance.  Terrible efficiency, but makes heat reuse a LOT easier (need less coolant/pumps, can boil water, etc ) 

The issue with large die sizes is yield.  If there are any defects in the silicon wafer, it ruins the entire chip, whereas small chips you can still get plenty of good ones out of each large wafer. 

https://x.com/i/grok/share/0676042956bf4d0a966afd08999945a2

👍1

The impact is on chip costs really. If you have a wafer failure rate of say 20% of its area, and you have 20mm ASICs instead of 10mm, you've effectively halved your yield per wafer.

**[#4433 · 2026-02-16 16:54 · Reckless Apotheosis]**

AI companies like xAI have wafer scale chips, but they also have $mm to throw into projects.

Miners can't validate much at this point. The total reported hashrate is not auditable. Afaik, only braiins tried to publish something that made it feasible. 

Hydrapool provides an API that the pool operator can choose to make public. This endpoint lets anyone download all the shares and this audit the pool.

**[#4447 · 2026-02-18 10:34 · Skot Bitaxe]**

the fan connector PCB footprints on the bitcrane PCBs will allow you to solder in the older 1x4 fan connectors or the new 2x2 fan connectors. It is a pain to unsolder these connectors, so choose wisely.

👍1

**[#4448 · 2026-02-18 10:36 · Skot Bitaxe]**

This is my sense of the stock fan connectors on Antminers (as always, DYOR)
- S19 XP and older: four 1x4 fan connectors
- S19k Pro: two 1x4 and two 2x2 connectors
- S21 and newer: four 2x2 fan connectors

**[#4998 · 2026-06-25 02:46 · jungly 🐕💸]**

Hey @AgentP137 (https://t.me/AgentP137) - just trying to wrap my head around gridpool. I have two big concerns - pool hopping and the loose consensus.

One thing that is not clear to me is gridpool's vulnerability to pool hopping. A miner can get a decent high difficulty share and then leave the pool to point the hashrate elsewhere. This is a classic pool hopping problem. We could put a TTL on the shares, but that will punish honest miners who won a high diff share and are still mining, but not being as lucky as in the past.

The same can be extended as saying, a large miner comes along, earns a bunch of slots and then goes away to mine on low variance pools. Then the small miners are working on gridpool to find a block where a no-longer-active large miner will get paid for small amount of total work done.

The other concern is the loose consensus approach taken in gridpool. You say in the docs that if a server finds its coinbase (or was it winners list) is not the same as the one received from the other server, it tries to sync with the remote server to reach consistency. This is exactly the consensus problem that sharechain solves in p2pool and was solved explicitly by using a consensus protocol in radpool. If your membership is known, I would recommend using a consensus protocol to reach coinbase and accounting consistency just like radpool did.

❤1

**[#4999 · 2026-06-25 05:43 · AgentP]**

👍1

**[#5000 · 2026-06-25 05:52 · AgentP]**

On Pool Hopping:  This always struck me as a strategy used by miners who don't understand statistics, I don't think the strategy you described works out in their favor.  But perhaps I haven't given the strategies enough careful attention.  The clearest reason I can point to is that in GridPool the block finder makes slightly more, because all miners take Slot-0 + all transaction fees for themselves on the templates they build.  If a miner hit a lucky streak and earned a bunch of extra payout slots, they should want to put point extra power into GridPool since that is their chance to actually mine a block that gives them an unusually high payout.
But I will dig into this a bit more to see if I can break it, it's worth understanding better

👍2

**[#5002 · 2026-06-25 06:05 · AgentP]**

On Consensus:  I've never heard of Radpool, but will look into it now, thanks!
GridPool definitely uses a consensus layer, just not a long chain.  In GridPool its analogous to the "heaviest chain rule", basically the "heaviest Winners List" rule.  This is actually a critical piece of the system.  If your node finds another node working on a different list than it's own, it asks for the complete proof of that list.  This includes all of the original PoW shares that were used to create that list, each with their own Slot-0 address for attribution and other header data so that your node can trustlessly prove that each of those miners actually earned that spot.  It also allows your node to total up the difficulty of all the shares that built that list (or this might be median, I can't recall, I'll check).  A higher difficulty list indicates a stronger team with more hashrate.  Selfishly, most miners would want to join the team that has the most hashrate and the highest chance of actually hitting a real block.  So if your node through gossip finds that there is a group of miners with a higher powered list, it will switch it's active list to match, and build templates that are consensus valid (pays the correct addresses) with that newer stronger team.

**[#5003 · 2026-06-25 06:13 · AgentP]**

This rule is also used to deal with two other important scenarios:
1/2 Latency:  An edge case is that half the pool sees a last minute share come in just before the next Bitcoin block, and thus include it in the new locked payout list. The other half, due to latency, sees the last minute share afterwards.  This creates a "pool split", which we'd rather avoid.  Both groups continue mining on the best list they see, but eventually word will get around of the stronger list.  This does result in some lost work, since miners on the minority list will spend some time producing shares for the weaker side.  To mitigate this, I added "Compact Share Relay".  For nodes that support it, shares are reduced to just enough info for other nodes to reconstruct the share proof (slot-0 address, nonce, time, version). This should be <1200 Bytes so it fits in a single UDP packet, FIBRE style.  This is still untested, but should help a lot.

**[#5004 · 2026-06-25 06:22 · AgentP]**

2/2 Censorship:  A naive miner might decide to try to censor the shares of another miner.  Though transaction selection is blinded, maybe they want to censor that miner's coinbase payout (Slot-0 address).  If they discard these "banned" shares, they will end up working on a lower difficulty list than miners who accept the shares.  As long as one node on the network keeps the shares, they will produce a list that is stronger (heavier) than the censored list, and most nodes should be economically interested in working for the strong team. The miner that "forked off" will start producing shares that don't include the correct payout list, and their shares will be discarded by the rest of the network.  I think this is even true if they had 99% of the pool's hashrate, not just 51%, although I think I need to game this out more carefully to make sure I'm not overclaiming.

**[#5005 · 2026-06-25 06:29 · AgentP]**

@jungly (https://t.me/jungly) Some of the ideas in GridPool are very transferable to P2Pool btw, so my highest hope is that GridPool existing, even just on paper, makes P2Pool stronger and better.  
For example: P2Pool could forgo trying to split transaction fees.  This would make it more censorship resistant, since txs could be blinded, and might enable UDP based compact share relay which could help address latency issues (I know you've got Uncle blocks already to help with that).
Also, allowing the block finder to keep slightly more of the reward to change the economics of block witholding attacks would work just as well in P2Pool as in GridPool.  The idea is fully transferable. 

But these are pretty significant decisions, and come at the cost of accepting slightly increased variance.

**[#5138 · 2026-07-22 16:11 · golana]**

The idea is basically to formalize the power policies at different layers.  The layers would be broken up into relative time scales, the higher slower layers would pass operating points/goals down to lower faster layers and the lower layers would implement them while enforcing constraints(think things like slew rate limits, valid frequency/voltage range).  The highest layer might be an economic layer that coordinates a whole site, the lowest layer might be the local controller on the hash board.

I'm working on a power aware source that takes in wall power and solar power and outputs DC voltage to power a miner.  It has an MPPT that maximizes the solar contribution to the output.  The idea would be for this source to talk to Mujina and tell it something like:  I have 1000W of solar and 1400W of wall power available and Mujina would use that information to set the operating points of the hash boards.  The policy that Mujina implements would come from the highest layer (maybe something like maximize hash rate or maximize efficiency) and be informed by the layer above it (available power and where it is coming from).  The hashboards controller would make sure the operating points are valid for its particular has board and then implement them, reporting any out of bounds requests back up to Mujina.

That's the basic idea anyway.  I started with just working on this power source design and then realized that a lot of coordination would need to happen to use the available power optimally.  For example if I if have an older inefficient miner that would normally lose money if I just let it run but I know my electric rate, power mix, and the operating range of my asics and how those affect efficiency and power draw I could set an economic policy of "please break even" and Mujina would decide how much wall power it is allowed to draw to make that happen based on the mining efficiency of different operating points.

❤3
🔥1

**[#5139 · 2026-07-22 16:54 · Ryan]**

This is good. It's helpful to hear everyone's use cases. There is a lot of agreement that we want it work like this, in the abstract.

At this stage, the more concrete we can make these examples, the better: here are the components in my system, here is how they are connected, here is specifically what I want to happen in each situation, etc.

Then we can distill those into the low-level, primitive mechanisms Mujina should offer. E.g.
• Given the mix of hashboards connected, Mujina should return per-board and aggregage curves of availabe power vs efficiencies, and control to setpoints on those curves.
• other

And we'll start at the lowest-level "layer" of mechanisms, and keep building with whatever we see fit to be in the base software.

At some height, we'll reach a point where maybe something doesn't belong in the base mining daemon anymore. For example, if one wanted a miner to subscribe to the local grid's hourly electric prices and adjust accordingly, should that be the mining daemon, or a second daemon that monitors the grid and uses the mining daemon's API? Perhaps two demons is the right architecture, AND we package both into the typical firmware image (OS) that gets loaded onto, say, an S19.

Lots of degrees of freedom to sort out. We should start with the primitives that belong in the mining daemon and cannot be done anywhere else.

👍2

APW12 have longer part numbers, like APW121215. this means it's an APW12 than can output between 12-15V. That's really what matters when powering hashboards. the letter suffix after the part number does mean some subtle differences in the PSU firmware.

**[#5188 · 2026-07-23 13:11 · Ryan]**

Interesting thought. I haven't seen any case where anything high-level like that has required onboard-microcontroller-like latency. In fact, unless something comes up which argues strongly against it, our goal is to keep the low-level firmware as simple stupid as possible, merely exporting the board's raw mechanisms up to the higher-level Mujina, which is more powerful, has all the context, and is much easier to update than on-board firmware.

**[#5201 · 2026-07-24 17:16 · golana]**

I didn't know there was a 120V unlock for the APW12, that's awesome.  I assume that I2C connection going from the PSU to the controller board has been fully reverse engineered and there is no current reading on it?  It does look like it has a voltage set and voltage read back though.  Have you monitored that read back voltage at different loads to see how much it changes with current?  I'm guessing the resolution probably isn't good enough to tell you much but it might be worth looking at.  It would also depend on the 12V rail staying constant under all types of operating points(other than current) as well since you are relying on it as the high side measurement when you calculate the voltage drop.  I might play around with this a bit.  It seems kind of interesting.

**[#5345 · 2026-08-12 08:51 · Marc]**

RescueMesh v0.1.0-alpha.1 — looking for open-source Bitcoin mining reviewers

We have published the first public alpha of RescueMesh, an experimental, free and non-custodial coordination protocol for privacy-preserving Bitcoin rescue research.

The current regtest prototype includes local encrypted storage, concealed transaction-set commitments, marginal miner-value simulation, signed discovery announcements, Proof-of-Help receipts and a loopback-only Stratum V1 primitive.

This is not a transaction accelerator and does not support mainnet, Bitcoin Core integration or production mining yet.

We are looking for technical feedback, especially on hidden-template validity, miner incentives, DATUM/Stratum V2 integration and decentralized discovery.

Repository:
 https://github.com/RescueMesh/RescueMesh

Release:
 https://github.com/RescueMesh/RescueMesh/releases/tag/v0.1.0-alpha.1

Documentation:
 https://rescuemesh.github.io/RescueMesh/en/
